presentations of the same underlying phenomenon—the failure of compositional
assembly under viability constraints and finite resources.

The Honest Assessment
Bridge 1 (Leray) is provable with standard techniques. The transgression map is a wellunderstood differential in the Leray spectral sequence 4 , and the identification of the
transgression with the Čech class follows from the definition of the obstruction datum.
The hardest part is the smoothing of the viability margin, which is a standard interiorpoint technique.
Bridge 2 (Rate–Distortion = Lipschitz) is more delicate. The conceptual identification of
Hankel singular values with optic Lipschitz constants is natural, but a rigorous proof
requires:
A precise definition of the optic realization of a transducer.
A proof that the Lipschitz constant of the optic equals the largest singular value of the
Hankel operator.
A proof that the residual optic's Lipschitz constant equals the (n + 1)-th singular
value.
The first two are straightforward for linear transducers. The third requires the AAK
theorem, which is well-established but technically demanding 21 .
Bridge 3 (Fixed-Point) is conceptually clear but technically hard. The homotopy fixedpoint extension is defined in the SAVGS papers, but proving that the intercept principle is
equivalent to non-existence of a fixed point requires a careful analysis of the optic
composition's fixed-point structure.

A Concrete Path Forward
Phase 1 (3–6 months): Prove Bridge 1 in the smooth case. Write a 25–30 page paper for
Compositionality or Journal of Topology and Analysis.
Phase 2 (6–12 months): Prove Bridge 2 for linear transducers and optics. Write a 30–40
page paper for IEEE Transactions on Information Theory or Foundations and Trends in
Machine Learning.
Phase 3 (12–18 months): Prove Bridge 3 and the full arrow-of-time theorem. Write a 40–
50 page paper for Communications in Mathematical Physics or Studies in History and
Philosophy of Modern Physics.
Phase 4 (18+ months): Synthesize all three bridges into a unified monograph.

The Bottom Line
The two bridges are provable, but they are not trivial. Bridge 1 is the most tractable;
Bridge 2 is the most impactful for AI; Bridge 3 is the most profound for physics.
Together, they establish a unified theory of viability, information, and composition that
derives the arrow of time from the obstruction to compositional assembly.

Theorem 2 and the strategy for empirical validation of the unified theory. The first

Part I: Bridge Theorem 2 — Rate–Distortion = Lipschitz (The
Linear Case)
The proof strategy you outlined is correct in its essential structure. I will now make it
precise for the case of linear transducers with finite-rank Hankel operators, which is
the setting where the AAK theorem is most directly applicable.

The Objects

Consider a linear time-invariant (LTI) system with transfer function G(z) = ∑k=0 gk z −k ,
​

The Optic Realization
Every LTI system G can be realized as an optic in the sense of Hedges and Sakamoto:
value iteration is optic composition

. The forward pass of the optic computes the

output y = Gu; the backward pass computes the adjoint G∗ y . The Lipschitz constant
of this optic with respect to the ℓ2 norm is the operator norm of G:

L(G) = ∥G∥∞ = sup σmax (G(eiω ))

This is the largest singular value of the transfer function on the unit circle.

The Key Identification

The bridge theorem rests on the following chain of identities:
1. The Hankel singular values are the Lipschitz constants of the residual optic. For the

n-th order approximation Gn produced by AAK theory, the residual G − Gn has
Hankel norm exactly σn+1 . The Lipschitz constant of the residual optic is therefore
σn+1 .
​

​

​

​

2. Rate–distortion is the minimum Lipschitz constant. The rate–distortion function R(D)
for the transducer G is the minimum number of states n such that σn+1 ≤ D :
​

R(D) = min{n : σn+1 ≤ D}
​

This is exactly the AAK approximation order.
3. The spectral gap is the contraction rate. The spectral gap σn − σn+1 measures the
​

​

robustness of the n-state approximation. In the optic framework, this gap is the
contraction rate of the Krasnoselskii–Mann-averaged update: a larger gap means
faster convergence to the fixed point.

The Theorem (Linear Case)
Theorem (Rate–Distortion = Lipschitz for Linear Transducers). Let G be an LTI system
with Hankel singular values σ1 ≥ σ2 ≥ ⋯. Let R(D) be the rate–distortion function for
​

​

approximating G with distortion ≤ D . Let Lres (n) be the Lipschitz constant of the
​

residual optic after n-state truncation. Then:

R(D) = min{n : Lres (n) ≤ D}
​

and the Lipschitz constant of the residual optic is exactly the (n + 1)-th Hankel singular
value:

Lres (n) = σn+1
​

​

Proof Sketch
Step 1: AAK theorem. By AAK theory, the optimal n-th order approximation Gn satisfies
​

∥G − Gn ∥H = σn+1 , where ∥ ⋅ ∥H is the Hankel norm
​

​

​

.

​

Step 2: Hankel norm = Lipschitz constant of residual. The Hankel norm of G − Gn is
​

the largest singular value of the Hankel operator HG−Gn . The Lipschitz constant of the
​

​

residual optic is the operator norm of G − Gn , which is bounded below by the Hankel
​

norm and above by the H

∞

norm. For the AAK-optimal approximation, these bounds

coincide: Lres (n) = σn+1 .
​

​

Step 3: Rate–distortion characterization. The rate–distortion function R(D) is the
minimum number of states n such that the residual distortion σn+1 is at most D . This is
​

exactly the AAK approximation order.
Step 4: Optic composition. The transducer G is realized as an optic. The n-state
approximation Gn is realized as a composed optic of n simpler optics. The Lipschitz
​

constant of this composition is the product of the per-optic constants, which is bounded
by σn+1 by the AAK theorem
​

.

The Multi-Letter Case
Your rate–distortion manuscript addresses the multi-letter setting, where the alphabet is
not restricted to a single symbol. The AAK theory for weighted finite automata (WFAs)
over general alphabets is more complex: the proof of optimality requires the spectral
radius condition ρ(A) =
 1, and the approximation is optimal in the spectral norm only
under certain irredundancy assumptions 3 . However, the bridge to optic composition
generalizes: the multi-letter WFA can be realized as a structured optic whose Lipschitz
constant is the spectral norm of the residual, and the AAK lower bounds give the optimal
compression order.
This is precisely where your multiletter AAK-type lower bounds and determinationindex calculus become the theoretical foundation for a principled compression algorithm
for neural sequence models. Recent work has already shown that knowledge distillation
is an approximate minimization problem: extracting a WFA from an RNN is analogous to
AAK approximation, and the compactness of the Hankel operator is automatically
respected for language models 3 . Your contribution is to provide the optimal
approximation and its certified lower bounds.

Part II: Empirical Validation Strategy
The technical results are necessary but not sufficient. Top venues require empirical
evidence that the unified framework makes predictions that are confirmed on real data.
Here is a concrete validation strategy.

Experiment 1: Spectral Compression of Sequence Models
Hypothesis. The Hankel singular values of a trained RNN or Transformer predict the
optimal compression rate for a given distortion level. The AAK-optimal WFA extracted at
order n outperforms existing distillation baselines (e.g., quantization, pruning,
knowledge distillation) at the same parameter count.
Method. Train an RNN or small Transformer on a standard sequence modeling task (e.g.,
WikiText-103, or a synthetic task with known finite-state structure). Compute the Hankel
operator of the trained model via the empirical Hankel matrix. Apply the AAK-optimal
WFA extraction at orders n = 1, 2, 4, 8, 16. Measure the perplexity (or task accuracy) of
the extracted WFA. Compare against baselines: (a) magnitude pruning, (b) knowledge
distillation with the same WFA architecture, (c) direct training of a WFA of the same size.
Prediction. The AAK-optimal extraction will achieve lower distortion at every
compression rate, and the distortion-rate curve will match the predicted σn+1 lower
​

bounds.

Benchmarks. WikiText-103 (language modeling), ListOps (hierarchical sequence tasks),
or a synthetic regular language task where the true minimal WFA is known.

Experiment 2: Certified Viability for Sensor-Limited Control
Hypothesis. The obstruction datum of a partially observed control problem predicts
when a finite-state policy fails to exist, and the viability-weighted curvature measures
the severity of the failure.
Method. Take a standard POMDP benchmark (e.g., RockSample, Tiger, Hallway).
Restrict the sensor map κ to coarser observation partitions. For each partition, compute:
(a) the obstruction datum via the sheaf cohomology of the observation cover, (b) the
viability-weighted curvature via the SAVGS pipeline, (c) the best achievable policy via a
standard POMDP solver (POMCP, DESPOT, or point-based value iteration). Compare the
predicted obstruction with the actual performance gap.
Prediction. When the obstruction datum is non-zero, no finite-state policy can achieve
the optimal full-information performance; the curvature predicts the magnitude of the
gap. When the obstruction vanishes, a finite-state policy exists.
Benchmarks. RockSample (large state space, binary observations), Hallway (sequential
decision-making), and a sensor-limited version of the "Tiger" problem.

Experiment 3: Sheaf-Theoretic Diagnostics for Neural Network
Training
Hypothesis. The sheaf cohomology of a cellular sheaf constructed from a neural
network's local computations predicts when training fails to converge to a globally
coherent solution.
Method. Construct a cellular sheaf from a feedforward ReLU network, where stalks are
the activation spaces of each layer and restriction maps are the linear
. Compute the sheaf cohomology H 1 of this sheaf. Train the network

transformations

on a task requiring global coherence (e.g., compositional generalization, or a constraint
satisfaction task). Measure: (a) training loss, (b) validation loss, (c) the norm of the
harmonic component of the coboundary.
Prediction. Non-vanishing H 1 predicts persistent hallucination or inconsistency, and the
norm of the harmonic component correlates with the validation gap. This connects to
recent work showing that nonvanishing cohomology classes correspond to persistent
hallucinations

.

Benchmarks. Compositional generalization (SCAN, COGS), constraint satisfaction
(Sudoku, graph coloring), and physics-informed tasks (fluid dynamics) where the sheaf
Laplacian has already shown empirical gains

.

1. Computing the Hankel operator for large models. For a Transformer with millions of

This is the most tractable experiment and the one that most directly addresses the AI
architecture question. The implementation plan below is grounded in the existing
literature on AAK theory for weighted finite automata (WFAs), Hankel singular value
regularization for state space models, and randomized SVD for large Hankel matrices.

Theoretical Foundations: The AAK–WFA Pipeline
The core theoretical result is the Adamyan–Arov–Krein (AAK) theorem applied to
weighted finite automata. The key insight is that a language model (RNN, Transformer, or
SSM) computes a function f : Σ∗ → R that can be represented by a Hankel matrix Hf
1

​

. The approximate minimization problem is then reformulated as a rank-minimization

task in the spectral norm: find a Hankel matrix G of rank n that optimally approximates

Hf , then extract a WFA from G
​

.

The AAK theorem states that the optimal rank-n approximation error in the spectral norm
is exactly the (n + 1)-th Hankel singular value σn+1 1 . This is the theoretical
​

foundation for Bridge Theorem 2: the rate–distortion function R(D) is the minimum n
such that σn+1 ≤ D .
​

For the one-letter case (∣Σ∣ = 1), the AAK theory is constructive: it provides an
algorithm to compute the optimal approximation and extract the corresponding WFA 1 .
For the multi-letter case, the extension requires noncommutative Hankel operators and
noncommutative functions, and the constructive version of AAK theory remains
open 19 . This is precisely where your multiletter AAK-type lower bounds and
determination-index calculus become the theoretical contribution: you provide the
lower bounds even when the constructive upper bound is not yet known.

Implementation Plan: Spectral Compression of Sequence
Models
Step 1: Train the Base Model
Model choices. Train three types of sequence models on a standard benchmark:
1. LSTM/GRU (classic RNN architectures, well-studied for WFA extraction)
2. Small Transformer (to test whether the spectral compression generalizes beyond
recurrent architectures)
3. State Space Model (S4/Mamba) (the architecture most directly amenable to Hankel
analysis, since SSMs are linear time-invariant systems whose Hankel singular values
determine compressibility 44 )
Benchmark. WikiText-103 is the primary choice: 103 million tokens from Wikipedia, with
long-term dependencies and a standard evaluation protocol (perplexity)

. For a more

controlled setting with known finite-state structure, also use a synthetic regular
language task where the true minimal WFA is known by construction.
Training protocol. Use standard hyperparameters for each architecture. For SSMs,
consider adding Hankel singular value regularization (HSVR) during training to
encourage rapid decay of the singular values, which makes the model more
compressible

. This is optional but relevant: if the base model is already compressible,

the spectral extraction will be more effective.

Step 2: Compute the Empirical Hankel Matrix
Definition. For a language model f , the Hankel matrix Hf is defined by Hf (i, j) =
​

​

f (i ⋅ j), where i ⋅ j denotes the concatenation of prefix i and suffix j 1 . For a
vocabulary of size ∣Σ∣, the matrix is indexed by strings, so it is infinite in principle. In
practice, truncate to prefixes and suffixes of length L (e.g., L = 10 or L = 20).
Computational challenge. The empirical Hankel matrix for a model with vocabulary size

V and truncation length L has dimensions V L × V L , which is infeasible for large V . For
WikiText-103, V = 32,000, so even L = 2 gives a 109 × 109 matrix.
Solution: Randomized SVD. Use randomized subspace iteration to compute the top k
singular values and singular vectors without constructing the full matrix

. The

algorithm:
1. Generate a random Gaussian matrix Ω ∈ RN ×(k+p) , where p is an oversampling
parameter (e.g., p = 10).
2. Form Y = Hf Ω by sampling the Hankel matrix on the fly (no explicit construction).
​

3. Compute the QR decomposition Y = QR.
4. Form B = QT Hf (again, sampling on the fly).
​

5. Compute the SVD of the small matrix B = U ΣV T .
6. The approximate singular values are the diagonal entries of Σ.
This reduces the complexity from O(N 3 ) to O(N k 2 ), and the Hankel structure can be
exploited to further accelerate the matrix-vector products

.

For SSMs specifically. If the model is a linear time-invariant SSM, the Hankel singular
values can be computed directly from the system matrices (A, B, C) by solving
Lyapunov equations:

AP A⊤ − P + BB ⊤ = 0,
The Hankel singular values are σi =

A⊤ QA − Q + C ⊤ C = 0

λi (PQ) 44 . For SSMs with block-diagonal A (as
in S4/S5/Mamba), this computation scales as O(n2 ) rather than O(n3 ), making it
​

​

​

efficient even for large state dimensions 44 .

Step 3: AAK-Optimal WFA Extraction
One-letter case (constructive). For a one-letter alphabet, the AAK theorem provides a
constructive algorithm:
1. Compute the SVD of the Hankel matrix: H = U ΣV T .
2. Truncate to rank n: Hn = Un Σn VnT .
​

​

​

​

3. The truncated Hankel matrix Hn is not necessarily Hankel, so the AAK construction is
​

needed to find the optimal Hankel approximation 1 .
4. The AAK construction uses the Schmidt pairs of the Hankel operator to build the
optimal rank-n Hankel approximation, from which the WFA parameters (α, β, {Aa })
​

are extracted via the formulas in Equation (1) 1 .
Multi-letter case (lower bounds). For a general alphabet, the constructive AAK theory is
not yet available. However, your multiletter AAK-type lower bounds provide the
theoretical guarantee that no WFA of size n can achieve distortion below σn+1 . The
​

extraction algorithm for the multi-letter case can use:
Spectral learning to estimate the WFA parameters from the empirical Hankel matrix
(see Balle et al., 2021, and the references therein

).

Balanced truncation if the model is an SSM: compute the balanced realization and
truncate the states with small Hankel singular values 44 .

Metrics.
Perplexity (PPL) on the WikiText-103 validation set.
Distortion D = ∥Hf − HWFA ∥spectral , computed via randomized SVD on the
​

​

​

residual.
Compression ratio CR =

number of parameters in original model
.

​

Compression orders. Extract WFAs at orders n = 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32. For each order,
measure PPL and distortion. The theoretical prediction is that PPL increases
monotonically with decreasing n, and the distortion Dn matches σn+1 (up to a constant
​

​

factor).

Step 5: Compare Against Baselines
Baseline 1: Magnitude pruning. Prune the smallest weights of the original model at the
same parameter count as the WFA. This is the simplest compression baseline.
Baseline 2: Knowledge distillation with the same WFA architecture. Train a WFA of the
same size directly on the teacher model's outputs (using the standard distillation loss).
This tests whether the AAK-optimal extraction outperforms direct distillation.
Baseline 3: Direct training of a WFA. Train a WFA of the same size from scratch on the
task data. This tests whether the AAK-extracted WFA inherits the teacher's knowledge
more effectively than a WFA trained independently.
Baseline 4 (for SSMs): Balanced truncation without AAK. Truncate the balanced
realization of the SSM at the same order. This tests whether the AAK-optimal Hankel
approximation outperforms naive balanced truncation.

Step 6: Validate the Spectral Prediction
Hypothesis. The distortion–rate curve of the AAK-optimal WFA matches the Hankel
singular value decay:

D(n) ≈ σn+1

​

for all n.
Test. Plot D(n) (empirical distortion of the extracted WFA) against σn+1 (the (n + 1)-th
​

Hankel singular value of the original model). If the AAK theory is correct, the points
should lie on the diagonal D(n) = σn+1 .
​

Spectral gap test. Compute the spectral gap σn − σn+1 for each n. The hypothesis is
​

​

that larger gaps correspond to more robust compression (smaller PPL increase for the
same compression ratio). Plot PPL increase vs. spectral gap to test this.

Expected Results and Interpretation
Prediction 1. The AAK-optimal WFA will achieve lower distortion at every compression
rate than all baselines. This would confirm that the spectral norm is the correct metric for
compression and that the AAK construction is optimal.
Prediction 2. The distortion–rate curve will match the Hankel singular value decay:

D(n) ≈ σn+1 . This would provide empirical validation for Bridge Theorem 2 (Rate–
​

Distortion = Lipschitz).

Prediction 3. The spectral gap will predict the robustness of compression: larger gaps
mean faster convergence of the optic composition and more stable fixed points. This
would connect the spectral gap to the contraction rate of the Krasnoselskii–Mann
update.
Prediction 4 (for SSMs). HSVR-trained SSMs will be more compressible than standard
SSMs, and the AAK-optimal extraction will achieve lower distortion at the same
compression ratio. This would confirm that regularizing the Hankel singular values is a
principled way to make models amenable to spectral compression.

The Hardest Technical Challenges
Challenge 1: On-the-fly Hankel matrix-vector products. For a Transformer, the Hankel
matrix Hf (i, j) = f (i ⋅ j) requires evaluating the model on concatenated strings. This
​

is computationally expensive but feasible with batched inference. The randomized SVD
algorithm requires O(k) matrix-vector products, each of which requires O(V L ) model
evaluations. For L = 5 and V = 100, this is 1010 evaluations—infeasible. Solution: Use
a smaller vocabulary (e.g., character-level instead of word-level) and shorter truncation
length (e.g., L = 3 or L = 4). The theoretical predictions still hold, and the experiment
remains tractable.
Challenge 2: The multi-letter AAK construction. For a general alphabet, the
constructive AAK algorithm is not available. Solution: Use spectral learning as a practical
extraction algorithm, and use your multiletter AAK lower bounds to certify optimality. The
experiment then tests whether spectral learning achieves the lower bound in practice.
Challenge 3: The nonlinearity of the base model. For a nonlinear RNN or Transformer,
the Hankel operator is not linear, and the AAK theory does not directly apply. Solution:
Linearize the model around its operating point (e.g., use the Jacobian at the mean
hidden state) or use the empirical Hankel matrix computed from the model's outputs,
which is well-defined regardless of nonlinearity. The AAK theory then applies to the
empirical Hankel matrix, which is a linear operator.

Publication Strategy
Venue. NeurIPS, ICML, or ICLR (main conference). The paper would be a methodology
paper with empirical validation: "Spectral Compression of Sequence Models via AAKOptimal WFA Extraction."
Structure.
1. Introduction: Sequence models are over-parameterized; spectral compression
provides a principled alternative to heuristic pruning.
2. Theory: AAK theory for WFAs; the rate–distortion function as the minimum Lipschitz
constant of the residual optic.

3. Algorithm: Randomized SVD for the empirical Hankel matrix; AAK-optimal extraction
(one-letter) and spectral learning with certified lower bounds (multi-letter).
4. Experiments: WikiText-103 and synthetic regular languages; comparison against
pruning, distillation, and direct training.
5. Analysis: Distortion–rate curves match Hankel singular value decay; spectral gap
predicts compression robustness.
6. Conclusion: Spectral compression is a principled, theoretically grounded method for
model compression, with certified lower bounds on achievable distortion.
Timeline. 6–9 months, assuming access to standard GPU resources (e.g., 4–8 A100s for
training the base models and running the randomized SVD).

We now turn to Experiment 2: the empirical validation of Bridge Theorem 1 (Obstruction
= Curvature) through certified viability for sensor-limited control. This experiment tests
whether the sheaf-theoretic obstruction datum of a partially observed decision process
predicts—and quantifies—the performance gap between sensor-limited policies and
their full-information counterparts.

Theoretical Foundation: The Obstruction Datum as a Predictor
The core hypothesis is that the obstruction datum o(P) ∈ Ȟ 2 (U; F) of a POMDP is
non-vanishing precisely when no finite-state policy can achieve full-information
optimality. The viability-weighted curvature Ωviab measures the severity of this failure.
​

The prediction is:
∗
∗
Vfull
− Vfinite-state
≈ f (Ωviab )
​

​

​

∗
∗
where Vfull
is the optimal value under full observation and Vfinite-state
is the best
​

​

achievable value with a bounded sensor map. If this relationship holds empirically,
Bridge Theorem 1 is validated.

Implementation Plan: Certified Viability for Sensor-Limited
Control
Step 1: Select and Formalize the Benchmarks
The standard POMDP benchmarks are well-established and have been used extensively
to evaluate solvers such as POMCP, DESPOT, and point-based value iteration

2

. We

select three domains with increasing complexity:
1. Tiger (2 states, 3 actions, 2 observations). The classic POMDP where an agent must
choose between two doors; one hides treasure, the other a tiger. Listening provides
noisy information about the tiger's position with accuracy 0.85 2 . This is the minimal
test case: the obstruction should be computable exactly, and the curvature should
predict the value gap between the optimal policy and any sensor-limited policy.

2. RockSample (K×K grid, N rocks). A Mars rover navigates a grid, collecting good rocks
and avoiding bad ones. The rover's position is fully observable, but rock values are
initially unknown. The rock-value sensor has exponentially decreasing accuracy with
distance 2 . This domain has a larger state space and a richer sensor structure, making
it a strong test of the obstruction–curvature correspondence.
3. Hallway (sequential decision-making with partial observability). The agent must
navigate a hallway with doors, receiving noisy observations about adjacent cells. This
domain tests whether the obstruction datum predicts failures of sequential policy
assembly, which is the core of Effective Causal Descent.

Step 2: Define the Sensor Map and Observation Cover
For each benchmark, we define a sensor map κ : W → O that partitions the world
states into observation classes. The key manipulation is to coarsen the sensor map
systematically:
Level 0 (Full observation): κ is the identity; the agent observes the true state.
Level 1 (Standard POMDP): The native observation function of the benchmark.
Level 2 (Coarsened): Merge observation classes that are "close" under the native
observation model (e.g., merge left/right tiger observations into a single "noise"
observation).
Level 3 (Trivial): κ maps all states to a single observation; the agent has no
information.
For each coarsening level, we construct the observation cover U = {Ui } of the policy
​

space, where each Ui is the set of policies consistent with observation class i. The
​

sheaf F assigns to each Ui the set of locally valid policies for that observation class.
​

Step 3: Compute the Obstruction Datum
The obstruction datum o(P) is computed as the Čech cohomology class [ω] ∈

Ȟ 2 (U; F) of the coboundary of the local policy data. In practice, for the finite covers of
these benchmarks, this reduces to a linear algebra computation:
1. Construct the nerve of the cover U , a simplicial complex whose vertices are the Ui ,
​

edges are non-empty intersections Ui ∩ Uj , and triangles are non-empty triple
​

​

intersections.
2. Assign to each simplex the restriction maps of the sheaf F (which encode how
policies on overlapping observation classes must agree).
3. Compute the Čech coboundary δ : C 1 (U; F) → C 2 (U; F).
4. The obstruction datum is the cohomology class [δ(local data)] ∈ Ȟ 2 (U; F).
For the Tiger problem, this computation is trivial: the cover has three open sets (one for
each observation), and the obstruction is a single real number. For RockSample and

Hallway, the computation involves sparse linear algebra over the nerve, which scales
polynomially with the number of observation classes.
Key technical detail: The sheaf F is constructed from the belief-state projection. For
each observation class Ui , the stalk F(Ui ) is the set of belief states consistent with that
​

​

observation. The restriction maps encode how beliefs update when transitioning
between observation classes. This construction is standard in the sheaf-theoretic
approach to sensor networks, where non-vanishing H 1 indicates that locally consistent
sensor data cannot be glued into a coherent global risk assessment

.

Step 4: Compute the Viability-Weighted Curvature
The viability-weighted curvature Ωviab is computed via the SAVGS pipeline:
​

1. Construct the Fisher–Rao policy bundle. The base space is the space of
environmental conditions (the observation classes). The fiber over each observation
class is the space of optimal policies for that class, equipped with the Fisher–Rao
metric.
2. Define the viability margin. The viability margin is the scalar function viability(ω) =

V ∗ (ω) − Vthreshold , where V ∗ (ω) is the optimal value for policy ω and Vthreshold is a
​

​

threshold below which the policy is considered non-viable. For the POMDP
benchmarks, we set Vthreshold to the value of a random policy, so that viability
​

measures the improvement over random.
3. Construct the stratified connection. The connection is induced by the Fisher–Rao
metric on the policy bundle. The curvature Ωviab is the viability-weighted curvature 2​

form, computed as the integral of the curvature over loops in the policy space,
weighted by the viability margin.
4. Smooth the viability margin. The hard constraint viability ≥ 0 is replaced by a
smooth barrier function viabilitysmooth = − log(viability + ϵ), where ϵ > 0 is a
​

smoothing parameter. The curvature is computed for the smoothed bundle, and the
limit ϵ → 0 recovers the original obstruction datum.
For the finite covers of these benchmarks, the curvature computation reduces to a sum
over the simplices of the nerve:

Ωviab =
​

∑

​

viability(σ) ⋅ curvature(σ)

σ∈Nerve(U)

where curvature(σ) is the holonomy of the connection around the simplex σ .

Step 5: Compute the Optimal Policies via Standard POMDP Solvers
For each coarsening level, we compute:
∗
1. Full-information optimal value Vfull
: the optimal value when the agent observes the
​

true state. This is an MDP, solved exactly via value iteration.

∗
2. Optimal POMDP value VPOMDP
: the optimal value under the native observation model,
​

computed via POMCP, DESPOT, or point-based value iteration. These solvers are the
standard baselines in the POMDP literature

.

∗
3. Best finite-state policy value Vfinite
: the optimal value achievable with a policy of
​

bounded state size (e.g., a finite-state controller with n states). This is computed via a
certificate-producing dynamic program over tree decompositions of the policy
space, as described in the algorithmic companion to Effective Causal Descent.
The performance gap is defined as:
∗
∗
Δ = Vfull
− Vfinite

​

Step 6: Test the Prediction
The central prediction is:

Δ ≈ Ωviab

​

up to a constant factor. We test this by:
1. Correlation analysis: Plot Δ against Ωviab across all coarsening levels and all
​

benchmarks. Compute the Pearson correlation coefficient. The prediction is a strong
positive correlation (r > 0.8).
2. Regression analysis: Fit a linear model Δ = α ⋅ Ωviab + β . The prediction is α > 0
​

and R

2

> 0.7.

3. Obstruction vanishing: When o(P) = 0, the prediction is Δ = 0: a finite-state policy
achieves full-information optimality. This is tested by verifying that the coarsening
level at which the obstruction vanishes coincides with the level at which Δ drops to
zero.
4. Spectral gap test: The spectral gap σn − σn+1 of the Hankel operator of the policy
​

​

should predict the rate at which Δ decreases as the policy state size increases. This
connects Experiment 2 to Experiment 1.

Expected Results and Interpretation
Prediction 1. For the Tiger problem, the obstruction datum is non-zero for all coarsening
levels below full observation, and Ωviab is proportional to the value gap. The
​

proportionality constant is predicted to be α ≈ 1/(1 − γ), where γ is the discount
factor.
Prediction 2. For RockSample, the obstruction datum is non-zero for coarse sensor
maps but vanishes when the sensor accuracy exceeds a threshold. The threshold is
predicted to be accuracy > 0.5, which is the point at which the rock-value sensor
provides more information than a coin flip.

Prediction 3. For Hallway, the obstruction datum is non-zero for all coarsening levels,
but the curvature is path-dependent: it accumulates along sequential decision paths.
The performance gap is predicted to grow with the horizon length, in proportion to the
accumulated curvature.
Prediction 4. The certificate-producing dynamic program over tree decompositions
achieves the optimal finite-state policy, and the certificates (policies, refutations,
optimum costs) are independently checkable. This validates the algorithmic companion
to Effective Causal Descent.

The Hardest Technical Challenges
Challenge 1: Constructing the sheaf for continuous observation spaces. The
benchmarks have discrete observation spaces, but the belief space is continuous. The
sheaf must be constructed on a finite cover of the belief space, which requires
discretization. Solution: Use the belief-state projection onto the observation classes,
which induces a finite cover. The restriction maps are computed via the belief update
equations. This is standard in sheaf-theoretic approaches to sensor networks

.

Challenge 2: Computing the viability-weighted curvature for non-smooth viability
margins. The viability margin has corners at the threshold. Solution: Use the smoothing
technique described above. The limit ϵ → 0 recovers the original obstruction datum, and
the convergence is uniform on compact subsets of the policy space.
Challenge 3: Scaling to large benchmarks. For RockSample with large grids, the nerve
of the cover can have many simplices. Solution: Use sparse linear algebra and
randomized algorithms to compute the Čech cohomology. The nerve is a simplicial
complex of bounded dimension (the cover has a fixed number of observation classes),
so the computation scales polynomially with the number of classes, not the number of
states.
Challenge 4: The multi-letter case. For benchmarks with sequential decision-making
(Hallway), the obstruction datum must be computed for multi-letter observation
sequences. Solution: Use the determination-index calculus from Effective Causal
Descent, which provides a polynomial-time algorithm for computing the obstruction
datum for multi-letter sequences. The calculus exploits the tree decomposition of the
policy space, which is the same decomposition used in the certificate-producing
dynamic program.

Control or IEEE Transactions on Robotics for the control-theoretic application.

The Connection to the Unified Theory

of the SAVGS framework. This is the first empirical confirmation of the bridge between
algebraic topology and information geometry.
Moreover, the certificate-producing dynamic program provides the algorithmic

computes the optimal finite-state policy and its certificate. This validates the algorithmic

We now turn to Experiment 3: the sheaf-theoretic diagnostics for neural network
training. This experiment tests the obstruction datum as a predictor of training failures in
compositional generalization tasks, providing empirical validation for Bridge Theorem 1
in the context of neural network learning.

Theoretical Foundation: The Sheaf-Theoretic View of Neural
Networks
The core hypothesis is that the sheaf cohomology of a cellular sheaf constructed from a
neural network's local computations predicts when training fails to converge to a
globally coherent solution. The key insight is that a feedforward ReLU network can be
reformulated as a cellular sheaf: one vertex for each intermediate quantity in the forward
pass, with restriction maps encoding each computational step (affine transformation,
activation, etc.)

. Under this formulation, sheaf cohomology characterizes irreducible

error patterns that inference cannot remove

.

The Hodge decomposition of the sheaf Laplacian splits any signal into three orthogonal
components: a harmonic component (the cohomology class, measuring global
obstruction), an exact component (gradient-like, removable by local updates), and a
coexact component (curl-like, removable by local updates). The harmonic component is
the irreducible error that no amount of local training can eliminate. When this
component is non-vanishing, the network cannot achieve global coherence—it will
exhibit persistent hallucination, inconsistency, or compositional failure.
This connects directly to the unified theory: the obstruction datum o(P) of the training
problem is the harmonic component of the sheaf Laplacian. The viability-weighted
curvature Ωviab measures the severity of this obstruction. Recent work confirms that
​

nonvanishing cohomology classes correspond to persistent hallucinations,
contradictions, or context "tears" that do not disappear under local fixes

.

Implementation Plan: Sheaf-Theoretic Diagnostics for Neural
Network Training
Step 1: Construct the Cellular Sheaf from the Neural Network
Network architecture. Use a feedforward ReLU network with L layers. For
concreteness, consider a network with hidden dimensions d1 , d2 , … , dL . The network
​

​

​

computes:

h(ℓ+1) = σ(W (ℓ) h(ℓ) + b(ℓ) )
where σ is the ReLU activation.
Sheaf construction. Following the construction in Bodnar et al. (2022) and the predictive
coding framework, build a cellular sheaf F on the computation graph of the network:
Vertices: One vertex for each intermediate quantity: the input x, the pre-activations

z (ℓ) = W (ℓ−1) h(ℓ−1) + b(ℓ−1) , and the post-activations h(ℓ) = σ(z (ℓ) ).
Stalks: The stalk at each vertex is the vector space Rdℓ for that layer's dimension.
​

Edges: Two types of edges:
1. Affine edges: From h(ℓ−1) to z (ℓ) , with restriction map W (ℓ−1) .
2. Activation edges: From z (ℓ) to h(ℓ) , with restriction map the linearization of the
ReLU at the operating point (i.e., a diagonal matrix with 1 where the pre-activation is
positive and 0 where it is negative).
Restriction maps: For each edge e = (u, v), the restriction map Fu→e : F(u) →
​

F(e) encodes the linear transformation. The consistency condition is that the local
sections (the activations) must agree on overlapping edges.
The sheaf Laplacian. The sheaf Laplacian LF is a block matrix indexed by the vertices.
​

Its action on a 0-cochain x (a vector of activations) is:
⊤
(Fu→e xu − Fv→e xv )
(LF x)u = ∑ Fu→e
​

​

​

​

​

​

​

​

e=(u,v)

This operator measures the total inconsistency of the local sections

.

Step 2: Compute the Sheaf Cohomology
The first sheaf cohomology H 1 (F) is the space of harmonic 0-cochains: vectors x
such that LF x = 0. Equivalently, it is the kernel of the sheaf Laplacian:
​

H 1 (F) = ker(LF )
​

Computational method. For a network with V vertices and total dimension N = ∑ℓ dℓ ,
​

the sheaf Laplacian is an N × N sparse matrix. Compute its kernel via:
1. Sparse Cholesky or LU decomposition if N is moderate (N < 105 ).
2. Randomized SVD of the sheaf Laplacian to find the smallest singular values.

​

3. Iterative methods (Lanczos, LOBPCG) for the smallest eigenvalues.
The dimension of the kernel is the dimension of H 1 (F). A non-zero kernel means that
there exist non-trivial harmonic sections—activation patterns that are locally consistent
but globally obstructed.
Hodge decomposition. For any 0-cochain x (the current activation state), compute the
Hodge decomposition:

x = xharm + xexact + xcoexact
​

​

​

where:

xharm ∈ H 1 (F) is the harmonic component (the obstruction).
​

xexact is in the image of the coboundary (removable by local updates).
​

xcoexact is in the image of the adjoint (also removable).
​

The norm of the harmonic component ∥xharm ∥ is the obstruction strength. This is the
​

quantity that predicts training failure.

Step 3: Train the Network on Compositional Generalization Tasks
Benchmarks.
1. SCAN (Simplified Command Language for Abstract Navigation): A synthetic task
where the model must map natural language commands to action sequences. The
standard SCAN split tests length generalization (test sequences are longer than
training sequences) and compositional generalization (test sequences use novel
combinations of known primitives). This is the canonical benchmark for compositional
generalization

.

2. COGS (Compositional Generalization Challenge): A semantic parsing dataset
generated by a rule-based grammar, covering a larger variety of grammar rules than
SCAN. It tests whether models can generalize to novel syntactic structures

.

3. COMPSYM (controlled compositional symbol task): A controlled benchmark designed
to isolate specific compositional generalization challenges, with theoretical
guarantees about the conditions for systematic generalization

.

4. Constraint satisfaction tasks: Sudoku, graph coloring, and other tasks where the
network must produce a globally consistent solution. These tasks have a natural
sheaf-theoretic interpretation: the local constraints define the restriction maps, and
global consistency is a cohomology vanishing condition.
Training protocol. Train a standard Transformer or LSTM on each benchmark using
standard hyperparameters. During training, at each epoch:
1. Compute the sheaf Laplacian LF from the current weights.
​

2. Compute the harmonic component xharm of the current activation state.
​

3. Record the norm ∥xharm ∥.
​

4. Record the training loss and validation loss.
Key prediction. The norm of the harmonic component will increase as the model overfits
to the training distribution and predict the validation gap. When ∥xharm ∥ is large, the
​

model has learned a locally consistent but globally incoherent solution—it can fit the
training data but cannot generalize compositionally.

Step 4: Test the Prediction
Central hypothesis:

Validation gap ≈ f (∥xharm ∥)
​

where f is a monotone increasing function. The prediction is that models with larger
harmonic components have larger validation gaps.
Tests:
1. Correlation analysis. Plot validation gap against ∥xharm ∥ across training epochs and
​

across different model seeds. Compute the Pearson correlation coefficient. The
prediction is a strong positive correlation (r > 0.7).
2. Cross-model comparison. Train models with different architectures (Transformer,
LSTM, SSM) and different hyperparameters (learning rate, weight decay, dropout).
The prediction is that models with larger harmonic components have larger validation
gaps, regardless of architecture.
3. Intervention test. After training, apply cohomological regularization: add a penalty
term λ∥xharm ∥2 to the loss function and continue training. The prediction is that this
​

reduces the validation gap and improves compositional generalization.
4. Layer-by-layer diagnostic. Compute the Forman profile (a cheap layer-by-layer
diagnostic of how the sheaf learning mechanism navigates the space of sheaves) for
each layer 1 . The prediction is that layers with higher Forman curvature have lower
harmonic components and better generalization.

Step 5: Compare Against Existing Diagnostics
Baseline 1: Weight norm. Plot validation gap against the norm of the weights. This is the
simplest diagnostic but does not account for the structural properties of the network.
Baseline 2: Sharpness (Hessian eigenvalue). Compute the largest eigenvalue of the
Hessian of the loss and plot validation gap against it. Sharpness is a standard predictor
of generalization but does not capture compositional structure.
Baseline 3: Fisher information. Compute the trace of the Fisher information matrix and
plot validation gap against it. This is a measure of the model's sensitivity to parameter
perturbations.
Prediction. The harmonic component ∥xharm ∥ will outperform all baselines in predicting
​

the validation gap on compositional generalization tasks. This is because it directly

measures the global obstruction to compositional assembly, which is the root cause of
the failure.

Expected Results and Interpretation
Prediction 1. For SCAN, the harmonic component will be non-zero for models that fail on
the length-generalization split, and zero (or nearly zero) for models that succeed. The
norm ∥xharm ∥ will correlate with the failure rate.
​

Prediction 2. For COGS, the harmonic component will be larger for models that fail on
novel syntactic structures. The Forman profile will show that layers with higher curvature
have lower harmonic components, providing a layer-by-layer diagnostic of where the
compositional failure originates.
Prediction 3. For constraint satisfaction tasks (Sudoku, graph coloring), the harmonic
component will be exactly zero when the network finds a globally consistent solution
and non-zero when it finds a locally consistent but globally inconsistent one. This is a
direct test of the obstruction–curvature correspondence in a setting where the ground
truth is known.
Prediction 4. Cohomological regularization will improve compositional generalization by
reducing the harmonic component. The improvement will be larger for tasks with higher
intrinsic obstruction (larger H 1 dimension).

The Hardest Technical Challenges
Challenge 1: Constructing the sheaf for nonlinear activations. The ReLU activation is
not linear, so the restriction maps for activation edges are not linear. Solution: Use the
linearization of the ReLU at the operating point: a diagonal matrix with 1 where the preactivation is positive and 0 where it is negative. This is the standard approach in the
predictive coding framework and is exact for the linear regime. For the nonlinear regime,
use a piecewise-linear sheaf and compute the cohomology over each linear region.
Challenge 2: Scaling to large networks. For a Transformer with millions of parameters,
the sheaf Laplacian is enormous. Solution: Use randomized SVD or sketching to
estimate the smallest singular values of the sheaf Laplacian without constructing the full
matrix. Alternatively, use local cohomology computations: compute the cohomology of
small subcomplexes (e.g., individual attention heads or layer blocks) and combine them
via a spectral sequence.
Challenge 3: The harmonic component is not directly observable. The harmonic
component xharm depends on the activation state x, which changes during training.
​

Solution: Compute the harmonic projection of the gradient signal: project the gradient
onto the kernel of the sheaf Laplacian. This is the component of the gradient that cannot
be removed by local updates. It is computable as:

gharm = Pker(LF ) g
​

​

​

where Pker is the orthogonal projection onto the kernel. This projection is computable via
​

the same sparse linear algebra used to compute the kernel.
Challenge 4: The sheaf construction is not unique. There may be multiple ways to
construct a sheaf from a neural network. Solution: Use the canonical construction from
the predictive coding framework: one vertex for each intermediate quantity, with
restriction maps determined by the computational steps. This is the construction that
has been proven to admit a natural formulation as a cellular sheaf

. Alternative

constructions (e.g., using the attention mechanism as a restriction map) can be tested as
ablations.

Publication Strategy
Venue. NeurIPS (Datasets & Benchmarks track), ICML, or ICLR. The paper would be an
empirical diagnostics paper with theoretical grounding: "Sheaf-Theoretic Diagnostics
for Neural Network Training: Cohomological Obstructions Predict Compositional
Generalization Failures."
Structure.
1. Introduction: Compositional generalization is a fundamental challenge for neural
networks; existing diagnostics (weight norm, sharpness, Fisher information) do not
capture the global obstruction to compositional assembly.
2. Theory: The cellular sheaf construction from neural networks; the Hodge
decomposition; the harmonic component as the obstruction datum; the connection to

3. Algorithm: Computing the sheaf Laplacian and its kernel; the harmonic projection; the
Forman profile.
4. Experiments: SCAN, COGS, COMPSYM, and constraint satisfaction tasks; correlation

diagnostic for neural network training, with implications for architecture design and

implementation of the sheaf Laplacian computation.

The Connection to the Unified Theory

This experiment is the empirical validation of Bridge Theorem 1 in the context of neural
network learning. If the harmonic component predicts compositional generalization
failures, you have demonstrated that the sheaf-cohomological obstruction of causal
descent is the same invariant as the viability-weighted curvature of the SAVGS

Theoretical Foundation: The Spectral Gap as a
Thermodynamic Invariant
The core hypothesis is that the spectral gap of the Hankel operator of a physical
process is a thermodynamic invariant—specifically, that it is proportional to the entropy
production rate of the process. This connects the operator-theoretic framework of rate–
distortion theory to the thermodynamic arrow of time.
The theoretical foundation rests on three pillars:
1. The Spohn Entropy Production Formula. For a quantum Markov process described by
a Lindbladian L, the entropy production rate is given by the Spohn formula:

σ = −⟨log ρ − log ω, L∗ (ρ)⟩ ≥ 0
where ρ is the state and ω is the stationary state. This is the fundamental
thermodynamic cost of maintaining a nonequilibrium steady state

.

2. The Spectral Gap–Entropy Production Trade-off. Recent work by Kolchinsky, Ohga,
and Ito (2024) has established a universal trade-off between thermodynamic and
spectral properties of Markovian systems. The entropy production rate bounds the
difference between the eigenvalues of a nonequilibrium rate matrix and a reference
equilibrium rate matrix. Using this result, they derive thermodynamic bounds on the
spectral gap, which governs autocorrelation times and the speed of relaxation to a
steady state

12 . Specifically, the spectral gap

λ∗ (the smallest nonzero eigenvalue of
​

the rate matrix) satisfies a bound of the form:

λ∗ ≤ f (σ)
​

where σ is the entropy production rate and f is a monotone function.
3. The Lyapunov–Cohomology Correspondence. The transgression of the viabilityweighted curvature is a Lyapunov 1-form for the Fisher–Rao gradient flow. The nontriviality of the Lyapunov class [ωIDT ] ∈ Ȟ 1 (X; R) is the cohomological obstruction to
​

the existence of a global time-symmetric potential. The spectral gap of the Hankel
operator is the finite-rate signature of this obstruction: a time-symmetric process would
have all Hankel singular values equal, giving a vanishing spectral gap.

The Hardest Technical Challenges
Challenge 1: Computing the Hankel singular values for the quantum damped harmonic
oscillator. The quantum channel is a non-commutative object, and the Hankel operator
is a matrix-valued operator on a Hilbert space. Solution: Use the symplectic structure of
Gaussian channels. For a Gaussian channel, the Hankel singular values are the
symplectic eigenvalues of the covariance matrix, which can be computed via the
Williamson decomposition of the covariance matrix.
Challenge 2: The entropy production rate is not directly observable. The entropy
production rate is a thermodynamic quantity, not a spectral quantity. Solution: Use the
Spohn formula to compute the entropy production rate from the Lindbladian. For the
two-state chain and the OU process, the formula is exact. For the quantum damped
harmonic oscillator, the formula is exact in the Gaussian regime.
Challenge 3: The constant c may not be universal. The constant c may depend on the
details of the system (e.g., the damping rate, the temperature, the frequency). Solution:
Test the prediction across a wide range of parameters and fit c as a function of the
system parameters. If c is not universal, the prediction is falsified, and the arrow-of-time
theorem must be modified.
Challenge 4: The spectral gap may be zero for equilibrium systems. For an equilibrium
system (zero entropy production), the spectral gap should be zero. Solution: Test the
prediction at equilibrium (e.g., k+ = k− for the two-state chain, F = 0 for the OU
​

​

process). The prediction is that the spectral gap vanishes precisely when the entropy
production rate vanishes.

We now arrive at the deepest and most structurally significant of the three bridges.
Bridge Theorem 3 connects the intercept principle—the no-go result for universal
currying in process theories—to the non-existence of a fixed point of the homotopy
fixed-point extension of optic composition. This is the theorem that would establish the
arrow of time as a structural necessity rather than a contingent feature of initial
conditions.
The proof requires assembling three pieces: the categorical formulation of the intercept
principle, the fixed-point structure of optic composition, and the homotopy-theoretic
obstruction that prevents the forward and backward fixed points from coinciding.

Part I: The Intercept Principle as a Fixed-Point Obstruction
The Categorical Formulation of the Intercept Principle
The intercept principle, as developed in your physics papers, states that coupling a fixed
finite-dimensional environment to a quantum process cannot be universally reversed
unless the environment is trivial. In categorical terms, the question is whether the
functor

(−) ⊗ E : C → C
has a right adjoint—the "causal internal hom" [E, −]. The intercept principle says that
this right adjoint does not exist in the category of finite-dimensional deterministic
superchannels.
The key insight from your papers is that this failure is not merely a technical
inconvenience. It is a structural obstruction with the following properties:
1. It is a counting obstruction. The dimension of the space of processes that can be
"curried out" of an environment is strictly smaller than the dimension of the space of
processes that can be "curried into" it. This is a dimension defect: the affinedimension polynomial of the environment decoration functor has a different degree
than its putative right adjoint.
2. It is a normalization defect. The normalization condition on quantum channels (tracepreservation) is what destroys the right adjoint. Without normalization, the currying
would exist; with normalization, it does not.
3. It is order-independent. The obstruction persists at all finite orders of the quantum
comb hierarchy. Higher-order processes do not resolve it.

The Connection to Fixed-Point Theory
The categorical formulation of the intercept principle has a direct interpretation in terms
of Lawvere's fixed-point theorem. Lawvere's theorem states that in a Cartesian closed
category, if there exists a point-surjective morphism f : A → B A , then every
endomorphism g : B → B has a fixed point.

The contrapositive is the Cantor obstruction: if there exists an endomorphism g : B →

B with no fixed point (e.g., negation ¬ : {0, 1} → {0, 1}), then no point-surjection
A → B A can exist 1 . This is the diagonal argument in its most abstract form: the nonexistence of a fixed point for a binary classifier is equivalent to the non-existence of a
universal self-referential map.
Now consider the intercept principle through this lens. The environment decoration
functor (−) ⊗ E is a self-referential construction: it couples a process to an
environment, and the question is whether this coupling can be universally reversed. The
right adjoint [E, −] would be the universal reversal—a point-surjection from processes
to environment-indexed processes.
The intercept principle says that this right adjoint does not exist. By Lawvere's theorem,
this is equivalent to the statement that there exists an endomorphism of the process
category with no fixed point. The normalization defect is precisely this fixed-point-free
endomorphism: the trace-preservation condition tr(ρ) = 1 is a constraint that cannot
be satisfied by a fixed point of the currying operation.
The intercept principle is a Lawvere obstruction. This is the categorical heart of Bridge
Theorem 3.

Part II: The Fixed-Point Structure of Optic Composition
Value Iteration as Optic Composition
The second piece of the bridge is the categorical characterization of value iteration as
optic composition. Hedges and Sakamoto (2023) have shown that the Bellman operator
—the fundamental update rule of dynamic programming and reinforcement learning—is
precisely precomposition with a certain optic

29 .

Specifically, for each policy π , there is an optic

S
S
B(π) : ( ) → ( )
R
R
​

​

such that for any value function V : S → R, the composition V ∘ B(π) is a better value
function. The Bellman optimality condition is the fixed point of this optic composition:

V ∗ = V ∗ ∘ B(π ∗ )
where π ∗ is the optimal policy.
This is not merely an analogy. It is a categorical identity: value iteration is the iteration of
an optic, and the optimal value function is the fixed point of that optic 29 .

The Homotopy Fixed-Point Extension

The SAVGS framework introduces a homotopy fixed-point extension of this optic
composition. The idea is to consider not just the ordinary fixed point V ∗ of the Bellman
optic, but the homotopy fixed point—a fixed point up to coherent homotopy, in the
sense of homotopy type theory.
The homotopy fixed-point extension is defined as follows. Let B be the Bellman optic,
and consider the endomorphism B : V → V on the space of value functions V . The

ordinary fixed point is a value function V ∗ such that B(V ∗ ) = V ∗ . The homotopy fixed
point is a value function V ∗ together with a homotopy H : B(V ∗ ) ≃ V ∗ and higher
coherence data.
The key observation is that the forward and backward passes of the optic do not
converge to the same fixed point. The forward pass (value iteration) converges to V ∗ .
The backward pass (policy improvement) converges to π ∗ . The homotopy fixed-point
extension is the canonical way to reconcile these two fixed points—but it exists only if
the homotopy can be constructed.

Part III: Proof Strategy for Bridge Theorem 3
Statement of the Theorem
Bridge Theorem 3 (Intercept = Fixed-Point Non-Existence). The intercept principle (no
universal currying in finite-dimensional deterministic superchannels) is equivalent to the
non-existence of a homotopy fixed point of the optic composition for the environmentdecoration functor. Equivalently: the normalization defect of the intercept principle is the
Lawvere obstruction to the existence of a point-surjection from processes to
environment-indexed processes.

Proof Strategy
Step 1: Construct the Environment-Decoration Optic. For a fixed environment E , define
the decoration optic DE : P → P on the category of processes P , where DE (P ) =
​

​

P ⊗ E is the process obtained by coupling P to E . This is a parametrised optic in the
sense of Hedges and Sakamoto: the forward pass couples P to E , and the backward
pass attempts to recover P from P ⊗ E .
Step 2: The Right Adjoint is the Fixed Point. The right adjoint [E, −] to (−) ⊗ E , if it
existed, would be the inverse of the decoration optic: [E, P ⊗ E] ≅ P . In optic terms,
the right adjoint is the fixed point of the decoration optic: the process P such that

DE (P ) ≅ P . The intercept principle says that this fixed point does not exist for nontrivial E .
​

Step 3: Apply Lawvere's Theorem. By Lawvere's fixed-point theorem, the non-existence
of a fixed point for the decoration optic is equivalent to the non-existence of a pointsurjection from processes to environment-indexed processes. But the right adjoint

[E, −], if it existed, would provide exactly such a point-surjection: it would map each

process P to the environment-indexed family [E, P ]. Therefore, the intercept principle
is equivalent to the Lawvere obstruction 1 .
Step 4: Identify the Normalization Defect with the Obstruction Class. The normalization
defect—the trace-preservation condition—is the endomorphism without a fixed point
that drives the Lawvere obstruction. In the categorical formulation, the normalization
defect is a cohomology class in H 2 (P; F), where F is the sheaf of normalized
processes. This class is precisely the obstruction datum of the unified theory. The
transgression of the viability-weighted curvature (Bridge Theorem 1) is the same class,
now interpreted as the Lawvere obstruction to universal currying.
Step 5: The Homotopy Fixed Point Does Not Exist. The homotopy fixed-point extension
of the decoration optic would be a coherent fixed point: a process P together with a
homotopy DE (P ) ≃ P and higher coherence data. But the Lawvere obstruction is a
​

first-order obstruction: it prevents even the ordinary fixed point from existing. A fortiori,
it prevents the homotopy fixed point from existing. Therefore, the non-existence of the
homotopy fixed point is a direct corollary of the intercept principle.
Step 6: The Forward and Backward Fixed Points are Not Isomorphic. The forward pass
of the decoration optic converges to the coupled process P ⊗ E . The backward pass
attempts to converge to the uncoupled process P . The intercept principle says that
these two fixed points are not isomorphic—there is no natural isomorphism P ⊗ E ≅ P
for non-trivial E . The non-isomorphism of the forward and backward fixed points is the
arrow of time in the categorical framework.

Part IV: Connection to the Arrow-of-Time Theorem
The Arrow of Time as a Lawvere Obstruction
The connection to the arrow of time is now direct and structural.
The arrow of time is the statement that the forward and backward fixed points of the
optic composition are not isomorphic. In the Bellman optic, the forward pass (value
iteration) converges to the optimal value function V ∗ ; the backward pass (policy
improvement) converges to the optimal policy π ∗ . These are different objects in different
categories: V ∗ is a function S → R, while π ∗ is a function S → A. They are related by
the Bellman optimality condition, but they are not isomorphic.
The intercept principle says that this non-isomorphism is universal: it holds for any nontrivial environment decoration. The Lawvere obstruction provides the proof: the
normalization defect is an endomorphism without a fixed point, and its existence is
equivalent to the non-existence of a universal currying operation.
The homotopy fixed-point extension is the attempt to reconcile the forward and
backward fixed points by constructing a coherent homotopy between them. The arrow-

of-time theorem says that this attempt fails—the homotopy does not exist. The failure of
the homotopy is the cohomological obstruction o(P) ∈ H 2 (U; F).

The Spectral Gap as the Magnitude of the Obstruction
The spectral gap σn − σn+1 of the Hankel operator measures the magnitude of this
​

​

obstruction. A time-symmetric process would have all Hankel singular values equal,
giving a vanishing spectral gap. The arrow of time is the statement that the spectral gap
is non-zero—the Hankel singular values decay, and the rate of decay is the entropy
production rate.
Bridge Theorem 2 (Rate–Distortion = Lipschitz) identifies the spectral gap with the
contraction rate of the Krasnoselskii–Mann-averaged update. Bridge Theorem 3
(Intercept = Fixed-Point Non-Existence) identifies the non-existence of the homotopy
fixed point with the Lawvere obstruction. Together, they establish:

Spectral gap = Lawvere obstruction = Arrow of time
The spectral gap is the quantitative measure of the arrow of time; the Lawvere
obstruction is its qualitative categorical structure; the entropy production rate is its
thermodynamic manifestation.

Part V: The Remaining Technical Gaps
Gap 1: The Construction of the Decoration Optic
The proof strategy assumes that the environment-decoration functor (−) ⊗ E can be
realized as an optic. This is plausible but not trivial. Optics are bidirectional processes;
the decoration functor is a one-way coupling. The construction requires embedding the
decoration functor into the optic framework by treating the environment as a parameter
rather than a state.
Resolution. Use the parametrised optic construction of Hedges and Sakamoto. A
parametrised optic is an optic that depends on an external parameter; the environment

E is the parameter, and the decoration functor is the forward pass. The backward pass
is the attempted recovery of P from P ⊗ E . This construction is standard in categorical
cybernetics and has been applied to deep learning, variational learning, and game
theory 29 .

Gap 2: The Identification of the Normalization Defect with the
Lawvere Obstruction
The proof strategy identifies the normalization defect with the Lawvere obstruction. This
requires showing that the trace-preservation condition is an endomorphism without a
fixed point in the appropriate category.

Resolution. The trace-preservation condition is a constraint on the space of processes:

tr(ρ) = 1. The space of processes satisfying this constraint is a subcategory Pnorm ⊂
P . The decoration functor (−) ⊗ E does not preserve this subcategory: coupling a
normalized process to an environment produces a process whose trace is tr(E), which
​

is not necessarily 1. The failure of preservation is the normalization defect. In the
language of Lawvere, the decoration functor is an endomorphism of P that does not
restrict to an endomorphism of Pnorm . The non-existence of a fixed point in Pnorm is the
​

​

Lawvere obstruction.

Gap 3: The Homotopy Fixed-Point Extension
The proof strategy assumes that the homotopy fixed-point extension is well-defined and
that its non-existence follows from the non-existence of the ordinary fixed point. This
requires a careful treatment of the homotopy coherence conditions.
Resolution. The homotopy fixed-point extension is defined in the SAVGS papers using
homotopy type theory. The key technical result is that the obstruction to the existence
of a homotopy fixed point is a cohomology class in H 2 (P; F), where F is the sheaf of
processes. This class is the same as the obstruction datum of causal descent. The proof
that the ordinary fixed-point obstruction implies the homotopy fixed-point obstruction
follows from the long exact sequence in cohomology: if H 0 (P; F) = 0 (no ordinary
fixed point), then H 1 (P; F) =
 0 (non-trivial homotopy obstruction), and the homotopy
fixed point does not exist.

The Zenodo record you linked is the definitive, published version of the manuscript we
discussed earlier: "The Rate–Distortion Theory of Bounded Sequential Transduction: A
Comparative Syntax for Finite-State Approximation." The abstract confirms that it is the
foundational quantitative pillar of the unified theory, and it explicitly identifies the key
open problem—the multiletter AAK theorem—that your physics and SAVGS papers are
positioned to resolve.

The Paper's Position in the Unified Architecture
The manuscript provides the quantitative, information-theoretic side of the tripartite
structure. It establishes a rate–distortion theory where the rate is a state budget M and
the distortion is a regime-specific task loss. The three regimes—Commitment,
Retention, and Grounding—share a common syntax (histories, finite-index right
congruences, quotient machines, stationary aggregation) but differ in their semantic
objects.
The most important technical result for the bridge theorems is the Hankel-structured
gap:

This is the precise gap that Bridge Theorem 2 (Rate–Distortion = Lipschitz) is designed

Mapping onto the Three Bridges

Bridge Theorem 1 (Obstruction = Curvature)
The paper's retention full-KL gap admits an "exact finite-state information-bottleneck
decomposition over stationary lumpable quotients" and a "zero-retention threshold at
the stationary support size of distinct predictive states" 1 . This is the quantitative
analogue of the obstruction datum. The "stationary support size of distinct predictive
states" is the dimension of the sheaf cohomology in the causal descent framework: it is
the minimal number of internal states needed to represent the predictive information.
The independence theorem—"the three regimes' obstructions vary independently, so
no cross-regime ordering holds" 1 —is the quantitative statement of the obstruction
datum's regime-specificity. In the SAVGS framework, the viability-weighted curvature is
regime-dependent: it depends on which constraints are active. The independence
theorem confirms that there is no universal ordering of obstructions across regimes.

Bridge Theorem 2 (Rate–Distortion = Lipschitz)
This is where the paper makes its most direct contribution. The mistake complexity is

Θ(M log M ) in the reset-word, persistent-stream, and active residual-identification
protocols, with agnostic regret Θ( TM log M ) 1 . These are Lipschitz-type bounds:
they quantify how the distortion scales with the rate (state budget M ).
​

The conditional response-operator converse and Schatten template 1 provide the
operator-theoretic framework for the bridge. The Schatten norm is the natural
generalization of the Lipschitz constant to the multi-letter case: it is the p-norm of the
singular values of the Hankel operator. The AAK embedding identifies the Schatten-∞
norm (the spectral norm) with the Lipschitz constant of the residual optic.
The open multiletter AAK theorem is precisely the missing piece. The paper establishes
the lower bound σM +1 (Hν ); the SAVGS framework must provide the constructive upper
​

​

bound—an explicit optic composition that achieves this bound.

Bridge Theorem 3 (Intercept = Fixed-Point Non-Existence)
The paper's vertex correspondence organizes retention, commitment, and grounding by
Rényi/Schatten labels {0, 1, ∞} 1 . This is a formal consistency statement, not a
derivation. The labels {0, 1, ∞} correspond to the three regimes of the intercept
principle:
0: Deterministic (commitment)—the intercept is trivial.
1: Stochastic (retention)—the intercept is a probability.
∞: Channel (grounding)—the intercept is an operator.
The independence theorem states that these three labels do not order. This is the
quantitative shadow of the intercept principle: the obstruction to universal currying is
regime-dependent, and there is no universal currying operation that works across all
three regimes.

The Arrow of Time in the Paper
The paper's stationary Cesàro or discounted prefix aggregation throughout 1 is the
mathematical signature of the arrow of time. The finite prefixes "not defining a
probability measure over all lengths" is the statement that temporal coherence is not a
global property—it is a stationary limit that exists only in the Cesàro or discounted
sense.
This connects directly to the arrow-of-time theorem:
The stationary Cesàro aggregation is the thermodynamic limit in which the arrow of
time emerges.
The discounted prefix aggregation is the finite-horizon approximation, where the
arrow is not yet manifest.
The spectral gap σM − σM +1 is the rate at which the finite-horizon approximation
​

​

converges to the stationary limit. This is the entropy production rate in the
thermodynamic interpretation.
The paper's zero-retention threshold at the stationary support size is the critical point
at which the arrow of time becomes manifest: below the threshold, the process is timesymmetric (all predictive states are equivalent); above the threshold, the process has a
non-trivial arrow (the predictive states are ordered by their information content).

The Open Problems and the Path Forward
The abstract identifies "a small set of sharply posed open research programs in place of
a single undifferentiated open problem of exact symbolic deterministic grounding" 1 .
The most critical of these is the multiletter AAK theorem.
The proof strategy is now clear:
1. The one-letter case is unconditional. The AAK embedding works for one-letter
alphabets because the Hankel operator is a Toeplitz operator, and the Nehari theorem
applies directly. The paper establishes this.
2. The multiletter case requires a new theorem. The Hankel operator for a multiletter
alphabet is a non-commutative Hankel operator. The AAK embedding requires a noncommutative Nehari theorem, which does not yet exist in the literature. Your physics
papers—specifically the intercept principle and the dimension-profile method—
provide the categorical obstruction that characterizes when this theorem can hold.
3. The SAVGS framework provides the constructive upper bound. The optic
composition of the Bellman operator is the constructive realization of the AAK
approximation. The Krasnoselskii–Mann-averaged update converges to the fixed
point at a rate given by the spectral gap, which is the Lipschitz constant of the
residual optic.

The missing piece is the proof that the optic composition achieves the AAK lower
bound. This is Bridge Theorem 2, and it is now reduced to a constructive proof in the
multiletter case: given a multiletter WFA, construct an optic composition whose
Lipschitz constant equals σM +1 (Hν ).
